![](images/ed8599fdfca4b30cd73e6b597c3d1fb93e1f7896ab1c242531102c5019398bd2.jpg)  
Figure 1: Trajectory flow through paired action–response state clusters for 100 retail/103 rollouts from Tau2-Bench. HDBSCAN over joint action–response embeddings yields one cluster assignment per rollout step; the first 30 steps are shown. Bar height encodes cluster occupancy, and bar color encodes empirical success probability (red = 0, yellow ≈ 0.5, green = 1). Within each ste<sub>p</sub>, clusters are ordered b<sub>y</sub> success rate from failure-associated (bottom) to success-associated (to<sub>p</sub>). ## 3<sub>.</sub>1 Ar<sub>c</sub>hit<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>u</sub>r<sub>e</sub>

Trajectory encoding. A learned linear projection maps each step embedding in AOS(τ) to $\mathbb { R } ^ { d ^ { \prime } }$ (d<sup>′</sup>=128), a bidirectional transformer encoder (L=2 layers, H=4 heads) contextualizes the projected sequence, and masked mean pooling yields a fixed-length trajectory representation $\mathbf { z } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d ^ { \prime } }$ . <sup>A</sup><sub>p</sub>- <sub>pen</sub>di<sub>x</sub> C <sub>repor</sub>t<sub>s a</sub>bl<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ons over</sub> th<sub>e enco</sub>d<sub>er</sub> d<sub>es</sub>i<sub>gn.</sub>

Prototypical classification. Given a few-shot batch S of l<sub>a</sub>b<sub>e</sub>l<sub>e</sub>d <sub>ro</sub>ll<sub>ou</sub>t<sub>s</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> <sub>a</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>,</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub> <sub>pro</sub>t<sub>o</sub>t<sub>ypes</sub> <sub>are</sub> <sub>compu</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d as mean re<sub>p</sub>resentat<sup>i</sup>ons, $\begin{array} { r } { \mathbf { c } _ { k } = \frac { 1 } { \left| S _ { k } \right| } \overline { { \sum _ { i \in S _ { k } } \mathbf { z } _ { i } } } } \end{array}$ f<sub>or</sub> $k \in \{ 0 , 1 \}$ (fail/<sub>p</sub>ass), where $S _ { k } \subseteq S$ <sub>con</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>ns</sub> th<sub>e suppor</sub>t <sub>ro</sub>ll<sub>ou</sub>t<sub>s</sub> of class k, and $\mathbf { z } _ { i }$ i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>represen</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>o</sub>f $\tau ^ { ( i ) }$ <sub>.</sub> F<sub>o</sub>ll<sub>ow</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> Snell, Swersk<sub>y</sub>, and Zemel (2017), a quer<sub>y</sub> trajector<sub>y</sub> $\tau _ { q }$ i<sub>s c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>e</sub>d b<sub>y a so</sub>ft<sub>max over nega</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> di<sub>s</sub>t<sub>ances</sub> t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e</sub> p<sup>rotot</sup>yp<sup>es:</sup> $\begin{array} { r } { \hat { p } ( y = k \mid \tau _ { q } ) = \frac { \exp \big ( - d ( \mathbf { z } _ { q } , \mathbf { c } _ { k } ) \big ) } { \sum _ { k ^ { \prime } } \exp \big ( - d ( \mathbf { z } _ { q } , \mathbf { c } _ { k ^ { \prime } } ) \big ) } } \end{array}$ , where $d ( \mathbf { z } , \mathbf { z } ^ { \prime } ) = \lVert \mathbf { z } - Within each task, stratified 3-fold CV over its rollouts: the <sub>p</sub>er-task discriminators (NC, RBF on the <sub>p</sub>ooled re<sub>p</sub>resentation; fastText on clustered state-ID sequences with n-<sub>g</sub>ram order n) are fit on each train fold (∼33 rollouts), while Precog-RM (<sub>p</sub>er-benchmark trained on all three models’ rollouts, A<sub>pp</sub>endix E) uses the same train fold as its su<sub>pp</sub>ort set, with no <sub>p</sub>er-task fittin<sub>g</sub>. Folds are identical across all methods; Precog-RM is additionally averaged over 3 training seeds. N = held-out test tasks per cell. • fastText (Joulin et al. 2017)<sub>,</sub> on the clustered ϕ: a linear n-gram classifier over state-ID sequences, swept from an order-free bag of states (n=1) to longer subsequences (n=2, 5).

## 6 R<sub>e</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d W<sub>or</sub>k

Representations of agent trajectories. Agentic trajectories are the central object of a growing set of analyses, including credit assi<sub>g</sub>nment (Fen<sub>g</sub> et al. 2026; He et al. 2026; Zhan<sub>g</sub> 2026), failure dia<sub>g</sub>nosis (Barke et al. 2026; Ta<sub>y</sub>ebati et al. 2026; Ou et al. 2025), and trainin<sub>g</sub>-data curation (Yan<sub>g</sub> et al. 2026). These methods o<sub>p</sub>erate on raw token sequences, handcrafted features, or prompted LLM judges, each designed for a single problem and committed in advance to the trajectory features that matter. Closest to our view, To<sub>p</sub>oCurate (Yan<sub>g</sub> et al. 2026) mer<sub>g</sub>es action-observation <sub>p</sub>airs from multit<sub>r</sub>i<sub>a</sub>l <sub>ro</sub>ll<sub>ou</sub>t<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o a per-</sub>t<sub>as</sub>k i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erac</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> t<sub>opo</sub>l<sub>ogy an</sub>d <sub>uses</sub> it to curate trainin<sub>g</sub> data. Related in s<sub>p</sub>irit, Sun et al. Where the compute goes. Generation accounts for 89% of baseline FLOPs and training the remaining 11%, which is <sub>w</sub>h<sub>y</sub> t<sub>erm</sub>i<sub>na</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> <sub>ro</sub>ll<sub>ou</sub>t<sub>s</sub> <sub>ear</sub>l<sub>y</sub> t<sub>rans</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es</sub> <sub>a</sub>l<sub>mos</sub>t di<sub>rec</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o</sub> t<sub>o</sub>t<sub>a</sub>l <sub>sav</sub>i<sub>ngs.</sub> Th<sub>e con</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>ll<sub>er</sub>’<sub>s</sub> f<sub>oo</sub>t<sub>pr</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t i<sub>s</sub> b<sub>es</sub>t <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>currency</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t d<sub>oes no</sub>t d<sub>epen</sub>d <sub>on</sub> $\bar { N _ { \mathrm { e m b } } } \mathrm { . }$ <sub>: em</sub>b<sub>e</sub>dd<sub>e</sub>d t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>ens are</sub> 2.4–3.0% of generated tokens in every configuration. Charged at policy parity, this costs 0.8–2.3 points of savings.
